However, Lord Brahma did not accept what the Pranava mantra said, that Lord Shiva is the Supreme Being, and he scorned it. At that moment, Lord Shiva suddenly took a divine form and appeared there like a huge golden mountain. Seeing this, the Devas, including Vishnu, bowed down. But Lord Brahma, seeing that divine lord, was not at all afraid and called Lord Shiva, saying, "My son, come here." Therefore, Lord Bhairava appeared from the divine body of Lord Shiva, who was angered. That Bhairava, thus appeared, plucked one of Brahma's five heads, who had committed Shiva-dushanam (blasphemy against Shiva), with his fingernail. By the grace of Lord Shiva, Lord Brahma, now free from delusion, stood up and worshipped and praised Lord Shiva. Brahma praised Lord Shiva and Goddess Parvati as follows: "O One who stood alone at the time of the creation of all worlds! O One who graciously created arrays of powerful beings to protect and rule the universes! O One who graciously returns to reside and merge all worlds! Support us with your grace." And then, addressing Ambika, he said, "O Daughter of Daksha, Ambika, Mahadevi, known as the daughter of the mountain king, the universal cause of Lakshmi, the Para Shakti! We repeatedly prostrate at your lotus feet to be freed from karmas and attain salvation. Protect us, your humble servants. The feet of you, the Divine Mother and Father, who have obtained all worlds without seeking them, are our only refuge." Thus, Lord Brahma offered his praises. Then Lord Shiva, pleased, addressed Lord Brahma, saying, "From this day forth, you shall be the Four-faced One and live happily without deviating from my command." After that, Lord Shiva, seeing Lord Bhairava standing respectfully with sharp trident and skull in hand, said, "O dark-bodied one, you, Kala Bhairava, are the cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution. You are also the subduer of all beings. You are one of my sons." And then, with divine grace, he commanded, "Go to the worlds of Devas, including Vishnu, accompanied by all these Ganas, accept blood sacrifices, destroy their arrogance, then remove the pride of the sages, and then go to Kanchipuram and perform Shiva Puja." Lord Bhairava did as commanded. He went to the worlds of Vishnu and others, accepted blood sacrifices, destroyed the arrogance of the Devas, captivated the minds of the Rishipatnis with his smile, destroyed the pride of the Rishis, and arrived at the city of Kanchi. There, he placed the Brahma Kapalam, from which he had accepted blood sacrifice, in one place and worshipped Lord Shiva, the Lord of the Vedas, by the name of "Bhairaveesan." He installed a Shiva Lingam and worshipped it. That Lord is named Bhairaveesan. That place is named Bhairaveesam. Lord Shiva, accepting Lord Bhairava's worship, bestowed his grace and said, "Beloved, as you wished, I reside here. You shall guard this city of Kanchi from before my shrine. Give this blood in the Brahma Kapalam for the Ganas to consume." Saying this, he disappeared. Accordingly, Lord Bhairava continues to guard Kanchipuram. This is the history of how Lord Bhairava guards Kanchipuram. We have thus meditated on the detailed explanation of the verse composed by Sekkizhar Peruman, through the Kanchipuranam. We will continue to meditate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 31 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 239, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman, having sung that Lord Ganesha and Lord Bhairava, the Sivakumaras, guard Kanchipuram, now sings that Lord Muruga guards Kanchipuram. Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Kundram Erindha Velavan Kaakkavum," meaning "May the Lord with the spear who hurled the mountain protect." Here, "Kundram" refers to the Krauncha mountain. This mountain refers to the deceitful Krauncha mountain, which was the abode of Tarakaasuran, who is described as the embodiment of Maya Mala (the impurity of illusion) among the three (Soorapadman and others). Just as souls caught in Maya wander helplessly within it, unable to find a way out, Tarakaasuran, through his illusion, caused beings who entered this Krauncha mountain to wander within it, unable to escape, and then destroyed them. The history from Kandapuranam states that Lord Muruga split this Krauncha mountain into two with his Velayudham (spear) and also annihilated Tarakaasuran. We can find the glories and this history in great detail in Kandapuranam. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that this Velavan, who hurled the Krauncha mountain, guards Kanchipuram. The inner meaning of Lord Muruga destroying the Krauncha mountain is that if the Maya Mala is destroyed, souls will quickly attain salvation. Furthermore, Lord Muruga resides in Kumarakkottam in Kanchipuram. The author of Kanchipuranam has sung its history in the Kumarakkottam Padalam of Kanchipuranam. When Lord Muruga was residing on Mount Kailash, Lord Brahma came to see Lord Shiva. Lord Muruga asked Brahma for the meaning of Pranava, and when Brahma did not know it, Lord Muruga imprisoned him. This history is known to all of us. The Shiva Ganas, upon learning that Brahma was imprisoned, went and informed Lord Shiva. Then Lord Shiva commanded Lord Muruga to release Brahma. However, Lord Muruga did not carry out that command. Therefore, Lord Shiva and Goddess Parvati went to Lord Muruga. The Velayudhamurti, knowing that his mother and father had come, bowed at their divine feet and praised them. Then Lord Shiva and Goddess Parvati placed Lord Muruga on their lap, rejoiced, showered him with affection, kissed his red-fruit-like mouth, smelled his head, and embraced him with overflowing love. After this, Lord Shiva smiled beautifully, caressed Lord Muruga's divine body, and said, "Kumara, even after I told you to release Brahma from prison, whom you had insulted, you disobeyed my command. To atone for that sin, you must perform penance. Kanchi city, which Maya fears to approach, is the best place to perform penance. Therefore, go to that place, install a Lingam named Devasenapathiswaram, and perform penance according to the rituals." Hearing this, Subramanya Swamy, adorned with a garland of Kadamba flowers, handed Brahma over to Lord Shiva with his Ganas and arrived in Kanchipuram to perform penance. In Kanchipuram, to the west of the Ulagaani Theertham, near the Kamakshi Amman Temple, Lord Muruga installed the Devasenapathisa Lingam and worshipped Lord Shiva according to the Agamic rituals. The place where Lord Muruga worshipped is called Kumarakkottam. In Kumarakkottam, Subramanya Swamy, Kumara Swamy, resides with a deer-skin garment around his waist, a sacred thread (mounji) adorning his waist, a Rudraksha mala and Kamandalam shining in his divine hands, and bestows grace in that form which removes the cycles of birth for those who remember him and grants them supreme bliss. Kanchipuranam states that those who once behold this holy place called Kumarakkottam with both eyes, even if they are of low birth, will be reborn as Brahmins in their next life. If Brahmins and others worship and adore, they will attain supreme bliss in the afterlife, knowing the nature of the Supreme Being, and live eternally without change. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings the history of how Lord Ganesha, Lord Bhairava, and Lord Muruga, the Sivakumaras, guard Kanchipuram. We have meditated on it in detail. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that Ayyanaar, the son of Harihara, guards Kanchipuram. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that Ayyanaar, holding the Chendayudham (a type of whip) in his hand and seated on an elephant, guards Kanchipuram. Ayyanaar, the son of Harihara, holds a weapon called Chendu in his hand. The weapon called Chendu is also called a whip. Its head is split into two and extends like a curved cane. That is called Chendayudham. The elephant is Ayyanaar's vehicle. The place where Ayyanaar, the son of Harihara, resides is called Chendanai Veli. This is the Vayali Street where elephants and horses are paraded. Even in Madurai, this Vayali Street is still called Veliveedhi. There was a king named Karikala Chola. He conquered kingdoms all the way to the Himalayas and imprinted his tiger emblem on the Himalayas, achieving victory. At that time, before his expedition, he worshipped Maha Sastha, the son of Harihara, who resides in Chendanai Veli in Kanchipuram, received the Chendayudham from Maha Sastha, and then undertook his expedition, winning all the battles he fought. The Kalingattuparani also sings about this. Silappathikaram also sings about this. "Kachchi valai Kachchi Kamakkottangavun mechchi inithirukkum meichchathan kaichendu kambakkalittru Karikaal Peruvalaththaan sempon giritharitta chendu" - this is a verse mentioned by Adiyarkkunallar in his commentary on Silappathikaram. Its meaning is that King Karikaal Peruvalaththaan received the Chendu from Maha Sastha, who resides in Kamakkottam in Kanchi, and struck the Himalayas with the Chendu. This verse is carved below the shrine of Maha Sastha, who resides in the Kamakshi Amman temple in Kanchipuram. We can still see it today. Thus, Maha Sastha guards Kanchipuram. The author of Kanchipuranam has explained the history of Maha Sastha guarding Kanchipuram in detail in a chapter called Maha Sasthan Padalam in Kanchipuranam. The name of that chapter is Maasaththan Thali Padalam. The history that Vishnu once took the Mohini avatar, and when Lord Shiva embraced Vishnu, Maha Sastha was born, is known to all of us. Maha Sastha, who became a judge establishing Vedic rules in all worlds and attained great fame, was born at that time. Then Maha Sastha praised and worshipped the lotus feet of Lord Paramasiva with his mouth. Then Lord Shiva addressed Maha Sastha, saying, "My son, listen to what I say. The glory you desire is attainable by my grace. If you wish to have true devotion towards me, you must perform Puja. Puja is Shiva Linga Puja. Kanchipuram is the suitable place to perform Shiva Linga Puja. Therefore, install a Shiva Lingam in Kanchipuram and perform Puja, and Devas, sages, and everyone else attained great blessings and supreme bliss. In such a city of Kanchi, worship and praise me. I will grant you the position of leader as you wished." Accordingly, Maha Sastha, the son of Harihara, arrived in the city of Kanchi. There, he bowed and praised the lotus-like divine feet of Goddess Kamakshi. He worshipped and praised Kumara Swamy, who holds the Velayudham, in Kumarakkottam. Having received their grace, he installed a Shiva Lingam and was performing Puja according to the rituals. At that time, Lord Shiva, intending to bestow grace upon Maha Sastha, appeared before him. Then Lord Shiva, pleased with Maha Sastha's Puja, bestowed upon Maha Sastha the title of leader of all his Ganas. He crowned Maha Sastha with that title. With countless warriors, elephants, and horses, Maha Sastha resides inside the Kamakshi Devi temple, guarding Kanchipuram, and also performs divine plays in the Chendanai Veli where he resides. Kanchipuranam states in the Maasaththan Thali Padalam that those who meditate upon and praise the Lord Maha Sastha, who is immensely powerful and has received blessings, will not suffer from poverty. They will experience happiness in this world and the next. Thus, we have meditated on the explanation of the verse sung by Sekkizhar, through the Kanchipuranam, that Maha Sastha guards Kanchipuram. Many deities thus guard Kanchipuram. We saw that Goddess Durga guards Kanchipuram, and therefore Kanchipuram is also known as Kannikkappu. Then we also meditated in detail on Sekkizhar's singing that Lord Ganesha, Lord Bhairava, Lord Muruga, and Maha Sastha, the son of Harihara, guard Kanchipuram. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that there are Shakti Yogis in Kanchipuram. Sekkizhar Peruman sings of "Tharpara Shakti Siddha Yogis." He sings that they reside in Kanchipuram. "Tharpara Shakti" means the Shakti who is the leader of all beings. It refers to Ambika. Sekkizhar Peruman mentions that there are Yogis who worship Ambika there. These Yogis are those who have worshipped Shakti. They are called pure Shakta sect followers. Their scripture is a Shakta text created by a soul residing in the Shakti principle. Their doctrine is that all inert and conscious worlds are manifestations of Shakti. These Yogis are Siddhas who consider Shiva-Shakti as the Supreme Being. Therefore, they are called Shakti Siddha Yogis. Not only that, but it also means that they possess the yoga that has mastered all Siddhis. Thus, Shakti Yogis who have attained Siddhi by worshipping Shiva-Shakti reside there in Kanchipuram. There are also Shiva Yogis, who are individual leaders possessing Shiva Yoga Sadhana. Furthermore, there are true ascetics who have attained long life there. These are people who have strengthened their bodies and attained long life by following Kayakalpa methods through medicine, mantras, and yoga. They also reside in Kanchipuram. Additionally, divine beings such as Siddhas, Vinjeyars, Iyakkars, and Gandharvas also reside there. Siddhas means those who have the power to perform Siddhis. They are referred to as the 18 Siddhas and their lineage. Thayumanavar praises their power as "Viththaga Siddhar Ganame." Thus, Siddhas, Iyakkars, Vinjeyars, and Gandharvas reside in Kanchipuram. Furthermore, Kanchipuram also has a Yoga Peetham where Yogis and Yoginis who perform yoga gather. As we have already seen, the Bhoga Peetham, which is the temple where Kamakshi Amma performs dharmas, is also there. Sekkizhar Peruman thus sings the glories and rarities of Kanchipuram, saying that Kamakshi Amma, who takes us into her grace, and who protects all six religions and all beings, has many such glories in that holy city. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that it is very difficult to describe all of them. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings the history of Para Shakti performing Puja according to Siva Agamas in Kanchipuram, the history of her embracing the Lingam, the divine history of Ekambaranathar, and many other histories we have seen so far. He sings all the glories and specialties of Kanchipuram. Having sung thus, Sekkizhar Peruman now begins to sing about the urban structures of Kanchipuram, including the city's layout and the rule of Karikala Chola. We will continue to meditate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 32 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 240, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman, who has so far sung about the events and histories that took place in Kanchipuram in various periods, yugas, and kalpas, whose exact time cannot be specified, now begins to sing about the history of Kanchipuram and its urban structure in the Kali Yuga. In the Kali Yuga, the king who ruled Kanchipuram was Karikal Peruvalaththan. His foot was charred by fire, hence he was given the descriptive name Karikala Chola. He was the one who established Kanchipuram in the Kali Yuga. The divine form of Karikala Chola is worshipped inside the Ekambaranathar temple. History states that Karikala Chola marched up to the Himalayas, conquered and subjugated all the countries, and imprinted his tiger emblem on the Himalayas, achieving victory. As he was heading towards the Himalayas, a hunter saw the glories of Kanchi and told Karikala Chola about them. Karikala Chola went to Kanchi, reformed the forests of that city, established the country, demarcated the city's boundaries up to four kadams (a measure of distance), made Kanchipuram a magnificent city, built walls, and ruled from there. We have already seen the history of Karikala Peruvalaththan receiving the Chendayudham from Maha Sastha in Kanchipuram and then undertaking his expedition. Sekkizhar Peruman, having sung thus, now sings about the moat surrounding the walls of Kanchipuram. Kanchipuram is filled with Kanchi trees. Kanchi flowers bloom there. The bees there sing as if singing the music of the Kanchi tune. Surrounding the walls of such a city of Kanchi is a moat. How is that moat? It is as vast as the ocean of grace of the Divine Mother. The Divine Mother wears an ornament called Kanchi around her waist. The beads in that Kanchi ornament sound the Vedas. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that this moat is as vast as the ocean of grace of Goddess Kamakshi, who wears such an ornament. The Vedas declare the abode of Para Shakti. They reveal Para Shakti. Similarly, the girdle called Kanchi, worn by Para Shakti around her waist, produces sounds and reveals Para Shakti to us. Therefore, that girdle is equal to the Vedas. Furthermore, it is also implied that the Vedas themselves are the Kanchi, the waist ornament, for the Divine Mother. These moats are built with walls, constructed according to the prescribed Agamic mantras, and with bhavana kriya (mental visualization and action). Goddess Parvati worships Ekambaranathar. Therefore, the walls of Kanchipuram were built according to the rules of the true Agamas, revealed by the Lord to protect this place of worship. Not only that, but the moat there illustrates the truth that it is very difficult for those who arrive in Kanchipuram, let alone others, to overcome their birth-related ailments. How so? It is as if the great ocean has surrounded Kanchipuram to remove its blackness and saltiness. Thus, walls have been erected to protect the place of worship where Goddess Parvati performs Puja. According to the Agamas, a moat surrounds it. When performing Puja, five types of purifications are performed to protect the place of worship. One type of purification is called Sthana Shuddhi (purification of the place). The five types of purifications are Atma Shuddhi (self-purification), Sthana Shuddhi, Dravya Shuddhi (purification of materials), Mantra Shuddhi (purification of mantras), and Linga Shuddhi (purification of the Lingam). The method of performing these is prescribed in the Agamas. This involves removing obstacles to the place of worship through appropriate mantras, visualizations, and actions, creating three walls shining with fire at the four corners, and three moats surrounding them, and similarly establishing the protective power above and below, thus achieving all five types of protection, which is called Sthana Shuddhi. There is also a five-fold purification in Jnana Puja. Sivakavimani Ayya writes that these are to be understood by listening to the teachings of those well-versed in Agamic texts. Just as ego and the resulting bondage of pasutvam (animal nature) are inherent impurities for living beings, blackness and saltiness are inherent to the ocean. Therefore, the ocean surrounding Kanchi as a moat to remove its blackness and saltiness is a metaphor containing the wisdom of the Shastras. Similarly, the inner meaning of this metaphor is that living beings can remove their impurities through their Shiva-related efforts. Therefore, the importance of Shiva Puja and its necessity are clearly explained here. Next, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the gateway of Kanchipuram. The gateway, or entrance, is positioned centrally. In front of a house, it is customary in our country to divide space for the nine planets and place the entrance in the section belonging to Mercury, who stands in the middle of the planets, believing that such a placement will bring prosperity. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the gateway of Kanchipuram is a central gateway because it allows all deserving beings who come to it to enter Kanchipuram. The path of Shiva, prescribed in the Agamic rules, is righteousness. All other paths are evil paths. This gateway allows Shiva devotees who follow the Agamic path to enter. This gateway also excludes those who follow paths other than the Agamic path. For this reason, this gateway is like the heart of great people. The heart of great people will not allow evil conduct, which is not Shiva-righteousness, to enter. The heart of great people will only allow good conduct, which is Shiva-righteousness, to enter. Similarly, this gateway exists in Kanchipuram. Just as the heart of great people is exalted, so is the gateway of Kanchipuram exalted. Just as the heart of great people remains impartial, so does the gateway of Kanchipuram remain impartial. Now Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the streets in Kanchipuram. Kanchipuram has beautiful streets with golden mansions. There are many streets like Manimaruga Neduntheru and Aavana Veedhi. Neduntheru refers to streets with ordinary houses, not grand mansions. Such long streets exist. There are also market streets where various goods are sold. All the beautiful streets in Kanchipuram divide the city into many sections like lines. Great mountains divide this earthly world into continents. Similarly, the streets of Kanchipuram divide the city of Kanchipuram into many continents, many sections. It is our tradition that there are seven great mountains that divide this earthly world. They are Himayam, Hemam, Nishadham, Neelam, Swetham, Srungam, and Gandhamadhanam. The continents divided by such mountains are nine continents. Those nine continents are Bharatha Kandam, Kimpurusha Kandam, Hari Varusha Kandam, Laviratha Kandam, Ramya Kandam, Hiranya Kandam, Kuru Kandam, Kethumala Kandam, and Bhadraswa Kandam. There are nine continents on this earth, including Bharatha Kandam. Similarly, when our ancestors built a city, they would design the city with nine continents, with each planet having its own continent. That is also a tradition. This is one type of urban planning by our ancestors. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about the urban structures of Kanchipuram. We will continue to meditate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 33 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 241, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the beauty of the streets of Kanchipuram. He sings that the arrangement of streets within Kanchipuram is like the divine Ganga descending to this earth and beautifying it by dividing into many rivers. In the streets of Kanchipuram, there are many tall mansions and palaces. The mansions have steps made of blue gems at the bottom and windows studded with diamonds at the top. This reminds one of the appearance of Lord Shiva when he stood as an immeasurable pillar of light, with Vishnu searching for his feet and Brahma searching for his crown. The blue steps at the bottom of the mansions are like Vishnu, who is of the color of a blue cloud. The diamond-studded windows at the top of the mansions remind one of the white body of Brahma. The author, having thus compared Vishnu and Brahma, has structured this verse so that we can infer Lord Shiva, whom both searched for, without using any comparison, because the Lord is incomparable. Therefore, it is not appropriate to compare him. Furthermore, the truth of the Lord, his existence, cannot be realized by direct perception (pratyaksha pramana) but only through inference (anumana pramana) and scriptural authority (agama pramana), which is the conclusion of spiritual scriptures. For this reason, too, the great author has arranged the verse so that we ourselves infer Lord Shiva without comparing him to anything. Chandrakanta stones are embedded on the mansions in the streets of Kanchipuram. In the evening, when the crescent moon appears, the light of the moon causes the Chandrakanta stones embedded in the mansions to melt and drip water. This is like devotees who, seeing Lord Shiva, who wears the crescent moon on his head, and worshipping Ekambaranathar, melt with love, their bodies shedding tears. These mansions are like those devotees, and water melts from the Chandrakanta stones embedded high up in them. Flags are hoisted on the spires of the tall mansions of Kanchipuram, and they appear to touch the clouds. The mansions are entirely built of polished stones. Because they are so tall and built of polished stones, the entire shadow of the universe is reflected in those polished stones. All animate and inanimate objects of this universe appear like shadows in the polished stones embedded in those tall mansions. This is like the precious and great treasury where Lord Shiva, at the time of creation, gathered all living beings to be given to Brahma for the work of creation. The divine history of Kanchipuram states that Lord Shiva taught the Vedas to Brahma and showed him Kanchi, instructing him to perform the work of creation. In the courtyards of the mansions and palaces of Kanchipuram, women play "kalangu" (a game) and ball games. The mansions appear like long mountains. The mansions are white with lime plaster. There are rows of stairs to reach the top of the mansions. The people residing in those mansions of Kanchipuram ascend and descend these stairs. It is as if they are ascending from earth to heaven and descending from heaven to earth. However, these stairs seem to be designed to distinguish between the people of Kanchipuram and the Devas. It is as if Devas are descending from the sky, and people are descending and ascending the stairs of those mansions. But since the feet of Devas do not touch the earth, these stairs help to distinguish between the people of Kanchipuram and the Devas, as the people of Kanchipuram ascend and descend them. There are streets where chariots run. In those streets, there are beautiful mansions. Next to those mansions are platforms. Blue gems are embedded in those platforms. The women of Kanchipuram adorn their feet with red cotton paste. They walk on those platforms studded with blue gems with their feet adorned with red cotton paste. Therefore, the red cotton paste on their feet leaves red marks, like lines, on the blue gems of the platform. This appears as if red coral branches are scattered here and there in a blue ocean. In the city streets of Kanchipuram, elephants walking create vibrations. Horses moving create commotion. Many chariots running create the sound of chariot wheels. The sound of anklets of dancing women in the dance halls of Kanchipuram's mansions reverberates. The trumpeting of Indra's elephant, Airavata, is heard there. The joyous neighing of the horses yoked to the divine chariot of the sun god is heard there. The rumbling of Brahma's vehicle, the chariot, is heard there. The sound of anklets of divine women dancing from divine vimanas is constantly heard there. Now Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the arrangement of streets, such as the Brahmin street, the King's street, the merchant street, and the Shudra street, in Kanchipuram. First, the Brahmin street. This street has houses where Brahmins live. The smoke of sacrifices always fills the air there. The smoke of sacrifices rises. Therefore, there are places of sacrifice there. Devas come there to receive the oblations offered in the sacrifices. Therefore, the mantras to invoke the Devas are always heard there. While all Devas, including Vishnu, wait before the temple of Ekambaranathar, the Lord of Devas, praying to Lord Shiva, the oblations to be offered in the sacrifices in the homes of Brahmins in the Brahmin street invite all those Devas. Next, the King's streets. These are large streets where royal families live. In their courtyards, there are spacious areas for weapon training. The streets for parading elephants and horses are very wide there. Royal princes are learning amazing skills like archery and swordsmanship there. All those weapons are kept there. Next, the merchant street. Merchants adorned with many jeweled ornaments reside in their houses in the merchant street. They possess immense wealth. Their houses are filled with gold, nine gems, and other treasures. The houses of each merchant are so grand that one might wonder if Kubera, who comes to worship Ekambaranathar in Kanchipuram, built them himself as Alagapuri to stay and worship Ekambaranathar, having understood the glory of Ekambaranathar's residence. Next, the Vellalar street. At the borders of Kanchi live great wealthy people of the pure Vellalar community. The two measures of paddy that Ekambaranathar gave to Kamakshi Amma, Kamakshi Devi gives to the Vellalars of Kanchipuram who worship her. Receiving it, they cultivate crops, increase agriculture, and live in such a way that all beings flourish. They also perform many charities and live as deserving of charities. The paddy, which is the root cause of the food given by Kamakshi Amma, is indeed great wealth. Truly, the wealth of the plow will not diminish, is an old proverb. This explains the greatness of the wealth of the Vellalars. Furthermore, it was given for the flourishing of all beings and for performing all dharmas. Therefore, it becomes the source of divine food. The source of food is paddy. Kamakshi Amma gives that paddy to the Vellalars, and they use it to cultivate and produce crops necessary for all beings. A principle called Beeja Ankura Nyaya has been prevalent for ages. That is, the question of which came first, the seed or the tree, has been asked for ages. In Kanchipuranam, Lord Shiva, as Ekambaranathar, gives two measures of paddy to Kamakshi Amma. With that, Amma performs all charities. She gives that paddy to the Vellalars, who cultivate crops and feed all beings. This divine history answers the Beeja Ankura Nyaya. The answer is that the seed came first. This is the answer to the question. Because in Kanchipuranam, it is said that the Lord gives two measures of paddy to Amma, Amma gives it to the Vellalars, and this world flourishes. Is it not mentioned in Periya Puranam? Therefore, the question of whether the seed came first or the tree came first is answered by Kanchipuranam and Periya Puranam, stating that the seed came first. Among the dharmas prescribed in the Vedas, agriculture is the most excellent. The farming profession, unlike other professions, involves the dharma of compassion for living beings. It is a cause for it. Therefore, all Shastras, including the Vedas, praise the greatness of the farming profession in this way. Sekkizhar Peruman, having sung about the Brahmin street, the King's street, the merchant street, and the Vellalar street, then sings as follows: All the caste distinctions that arose from inappropriate unions within these four high castes, both high and low, reside in separate places suited to their status. Furthermore, they live according to their traditional occupations, mingling with their relatives. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that all such large settlements are located outside Kanchipuram. Sekkizhar Peruman further sings: In this ancient abode of Lord Shiva, the city of Kanchi, festivals are always ongoing. Lamps and incense always fill the air. Flags are always hoisted, and festivals are continuously celebrated. Various musical instruments, such as the Beri, are constantly played during the festivals in Lord Shiva's temple. The sounds of these various instruments, including the Beri, played during festivals, prevent evil spirits, ghosts, and the troubles they cause from afflicting the people. The sounds of these instruments, such as the Beri, have the power to protect people from such troubles. Furthermore, festivals are always ongoing there. Therefore, Lord Shiva's Rishabha flag is always flying. The Shiva Agamas assert that the Rishabha flag has the power to remove the evil of the bonds (pasam) of living beings. Toranas (festive arches) are always present at the entrances of the mansions in Kanchipuram. Auspicious events are always taking place in the houses there. Devotees are constantly circumambulating the temple. Crowds of Devas also fill the place. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that there are no faults in Kanchipuram. The people in Kanchipuram are like Devas. They conquer people from other countries and engage in war games. Siddhas, through the practice of Kayakalpa methods, have attained very long lives. Similarly, the people living in Kanchipuram also live long lives like those Siddhas. Thus, Kanchipuram, where Lord Shiva resides with the divine name Ekambaranathar, has attained very great fame. Kanchipuram is like a vessel filled with all the resources that lead to the excellence of countless enjoyments in all 14 worlds. Sekkizhar Peruman, having thus sung the glories of the city of Kanchipuram, now begins to narrate the history of Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar from the 111th verse onwards. We will continue to meditate. Thirukurippu.

Chapter 34 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 242, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman, having so far sung about the glories of Kanchipuram, the glories of Thondai Nadu, and so on, now begins to narrate the history of Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar. In that holy city of Kanchi, which possesses all the glories we have seen so far, our Nayanar, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar, lived. Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar belonged to the Ekaali (washerman) community, one of the lineages mentioned in this world. Therefore, according to that tradition, he lived in a secluded part of the great city of Kanchi, in a designated area. There is an implicit meaning here that he lived in a secluded place, such that the world would not know his greatness unless the Lord revealed it. The Nayanar, in terms of his worldly birth and physical association, belonged to the Ekaali community. Ekaali means a person belonging to the washerman community. One must distinguish between the state of the soul and the state of the body. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that he belonged to the Ekaali community by birth to emphasize this distinction. Similarly, we saw Sekkizhar Peruman singing that Thiruneelakanta Kuyavanar Nayanar belonged to the Vetkover community. Likewise, in the Eyarpagai Nayanar Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman sang in the 405th verse that Eyarpagai Nayanar was a "Kul Mudhal Vanigar" (a chief merchant of his lineage). In the Amarnithi Nayanar Puranam, we saw Sekkizhar Peruman singing in the 503rd verse that Amarnithi Nayanar belonged to the merchant community. In the Kungiliya Kalaya Nayanar Puranam, we saw in the 835th verse that Kalaya Nayanar's lineage was described as "Andhanar Kalayar" (Brahmin Kalayar). Here, our Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar belonged to the Ekaali community, the washerman lineage. He used to offer Thumbai flowers to Lord Shiva. Therefore, the traditional name "Thumbaiyaan" is prevalent in common usage. Sivakavimani Ayya writes that this information was conveyed by an ardent devotee from that lineage, Thiru Muthu from Thanjavur. Our Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar possessed unwavering devotion to Lord Shiva. Sekkizhar describes it as "Sevviya Anbu" (perfect love). "Sevviya" means perfect or leading to liberation. Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar lived a life of Saiva discipline with perfect love. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar in this verse as "Adi Vazhi Thondar" (a devotee who follows the path of the Lord's feet). This means he and his family were hereditary servants of Lord Shiva's feet. This implies that his ancestors also performed service to Shiva. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman begins the Puranam of Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar. In the Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman narrates the history of the Nayanar in 18 verses, from the 1188th verse to the 1205th verse. There are 18 Puranas. Thirugnanasambandar Peruman refers to these as "Soodhan Oli Maalai" in Thiruvachagam. It is evident from Sekkizhar Peruman narrating the Nayanar's history in 18 verses that this Puranam is an explanation of Shiva Punya, which is the essence of these 18 Mahapuranas. Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar was one who dedicated his three instruments – mind, speech, and body – entirely to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva and to those associated with them. Mind, speech, and body are called the three instruments. Among these, the mind is the instrument that prompts from within, so it is given primary importance, and the three instruments are listed as mind, speech, and body. What is the purpose of our birth on this earth? It is to keep these three instruments dedicated to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. Appar Swamigal sings, "Vazhththa Vaayum Ninaikka Mada Nenjum Thaazhththa Senniyum Thantha Thalaivan" (The Lord who gave us a mouth to praise, a foolish mind to think, and a head to bow). Appar Swamigal states that the Lord gave us the mind to think of Him, the mouth to praise Him, and the head to bow and worship before Him. This is the purpose of birth. The mind should think of Lord Shiva and His devotees. The mouth should chant the sacred five-letter mantra of Lord Shiva. It should praise the devotees of Lord Shiva. The body, called "mey," should serve Lord Shiva and His devotees. Doing so means dedicating oneself to the Lord's feet. Manikkavasagar Swamigal also sings in Thiruvachagam, "Sinthana Ninthanakku Aakki Naayinen Than Kanninai Nin Thiruppaatha Pothukku Aakki Vanthanaiyum Malarkke Aakki Vaakku Un Mani Vaartthaikku Aakki" (Making my thoughts yours, O Lord, making my eyes for your lotus feet, making my worship for your flowers, and my speech for your precious words). This is the 26th verse in the Thiruchathagam section of Thiruvachagam. Thus, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar lived by dedicating his mind, speech, and body – the three instruments – solely to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. To live by dedicating oneself to the Lord's feet means that if one understands and honors the unspoken intention of the Lord's true devotees, that itself is dedication to Lord Shiva's sacred feet. Sekkizhar Peruman further explains this. Shiva's devotees are true devotees, excellent in Shiva Punya. Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar had the quality of understanding the unspoken intention of these Shiva devotees through their subtle indications and fulfilling their wishes even before they expressed them. Because he understood the unspoken intention of the devotees through subtle indications and performed service, the name Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar was given to him. The Nayanar's original name is not known to us. Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar is a special name he earned due to his steadfastness and character. What does it mean to honor Shiva's devotees? It means praising Shiva's devotees, doing what Shiva's devotees commanded, and doing what Shiva's devotees intended even without being commanded. The Nayanar performed all these actions. That is what is meant by honoring Shiva's devotees here. The city of Kanchi is renowned in all directions, with the sound of chariots and the neighing of horses echoing. In such a city of Kanchi, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar performs true servitude to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. He was a common washerman who washed clothes for all the townspeople. However, those who knew the Nayanar's service to Shiva and his service to devotees would not dare to call him a common washerman. Our Nayanar was a person of such great distinction and glory. Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar would wash the clothes of Shiva's devotees with great joy. Shiva's devotees are those whose hearts melt upon hearing the name of Lord Shiva. "Vellanthal virisadaiyai vidaiyai vinnorkku perumane enakkettu vetta nenjai pallanthaluru punalil keezh melaga pathaithu urugum avar" - this is from Thiruchathagam, Thiruvachagam. Thus, he performed the service of washing the clothes of Shiva's devotees, whose hearts melted upon hearing the name of Lord Shiva, removing the dirt from their clothes, and returning them. All other people of the Nayanar's community, who lived in his lineage, performed the work of washing clothes as a profession to earn their livelihood and obtain food. But our Nayanar transformed his traditional profession into a service to Lord Shiva and His devotees. Furthermore, the Nayanar performed this service with pleasure. Sekkizhar Peruman further sings that just as he removed the specks of dirt from the clothes of Shiva's devotees, he lived in a way that shed the dirt of his great cycle of births. The sacred feet of Lord Shiva are radiant. Those sacred feet remove the inner darkness of ego from the devotees who reach them. Would the clothes worn by such devotees of Lord Shiva be stained? No, the clothes of devotees would not be stained. But there would be specks of dust on them. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings "thoosudaiya thugal maasu" (dusty specks of dirt) instead of "thoosudaiya maasu" (dusty dirt). Those who have true devotion to Lord Shiva's feet are Shiva's devotees. They are devotees of the sacred feet. Therefore, their clothes would never be stained. But there would be specks of dust on them. Therefore, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar removing the dirt from the clothes of devotees and washing them and returning them is truly interpreted as him removing the dirt of his great cycle of births. The cause of births is the three impurities called Anavam (ego), Kanmam (karma), and Mayai (illusion). Birth is a consequence of these three impurities. Therefore, this birth is an impurity for the soul and conceals it. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to these three impurities as "maasu" (dirt) here. How to remove these impurities, these three malas, that cling to the soul? It is only through Shiva Punya, the spiritual practice of serving Lord Shiva and His devotees, that one can remove the impurity of birth. Thus, Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar removing the specks of dust from the clothes of Shiva's devotees truly means that he removed the dust, the impurity, of his great cycle of births. The beautiful daughter, Goddess Parvati, born to the king of the golden Himalayas, performed penance to reveal her ascetic state to the world, and the Lord bestowed His grace in Kanchipuram. Similarly, Lord Shiva decided to bestow grace upon His devotees, seeing the unparalleled devotion of His devotees, and to reveal the Nayanar's ascetic state and his greatness to the world, the Lord decided to take him into His grace. The Lord Shiva, with a very emaciated body due to the cold, wearing dirty, patched clothes, took the form of a great ascetic, a Shiva devotee, and came to Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar. Lord Shiva, in the guise of a Shiva devotee, came to Thirukurippu Tonda Nayanar, walking with small steps, His lotus-like feet, unknown even to Vishnu, appearing before the Nayanar, who had a flawless mind. We will continue to meditate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 35 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 243. Mummaiyal Ulaganda Sarukkam. The Puranam of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. Before Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, Lord Shiva appears in the guise of a devotee of Shiva. He comes before the Nayanar, wearing dirty rags on his sacred body, with a emaciated form, bearing the sacred guise of Shiva, which is a form of great penance. The clothes worn by Shiva's devotees are called 'kandai' (rags). 'Kandai' refers to a garment like a quilt, made by stitching together four or five layers of fabric with threads. 'Kandai' also has another meaning: torn clothes. Wearing such ragged clothes, he comes in the sacred guise of a Shiva devotee, adorned with sacred ash, rudraksha, and matted hair, in a form of great penance. With a very emaciated body, wearing these dirty rags, he walks with faltering steps, befitting the guise he has assumed. The time of his arrival is described by Sekkizhar as 'Seethamali Kalam,' meaning a period of intense cold. The sacred day of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's liberation is Chithirai month, Swathi star. However, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to it here as a period of intense cold. Therefore, it is understood that even in Chithirai, there were days in the past with heavy rainfall, and days when heavy rains in Chithirai brought intense cold. Thus, Lord Shiva comes in the guise of a Shiva devotee, with dirty rags covering his sacred body, which is radiant with sacred ash, like a black cloud covering a bright form. He appears with a radiant sacred body. Sekkizhar Peruman compares the dirty rags worn by the Shiva devotee to a dark black cloud. We all know that a dark black cloud indicates impending rain. Here, that sacred form is the cause for the great rain of the world and the rain of great grace to fall upon Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman compares the clothes worn by the Shiva devotee on his sacred body to a dark cloud. Upon seeing such a Shiva devotee, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's hair stands on end. Seeing the devotee, he rejoices, bows, prostrates, and rises. When seeing, speaking to, or thinking of great people and deities, goosebumps are a prime characteristic of devotion. Thus, upon seeing the Shiva devotee, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar approaches him and, with many sweet words, asks with folded hands, "O great ascetic, what is the reason for your emaciated sacred body?" The Shiva devotee comes before Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar with a slow, faltering gait. Therefore, recognizing the devotee's condition, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar speaks sweet words to him, asking the reason for his emaciated sacred body. The Shiva devotee comes with dirty rags before Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, who is an Ekali (washerman) dedicated to serving devotees. Lord Shiva, who is Digambara (naked), makes his unsoiled clothes appear soiled. This is because Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's profession is washing clothes. Thus, he comes with soiled clothes to cleanse Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's birth through his profession of washing clothes. Furthermore, he conceals his blue throat. If he had revealed his blue throat, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar would have prayed to Lord Shiva to cleanse the impurities of his birth. Therefore, without doing so, Lord Shiva comes in the guise of a Shiva devotee with the intention of removing and cleansing Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's birth impurities through the profession he had undertaken.

He says, "The clothes of that Shiva devotee are ragged and full of dirt particles." Therefore, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, with folded hands, humbly requests the Shiva devotee, "Please give me those ragged clothes you are wearing so that I may wash them for you." The Shiva devotee who had come replied, "These rags I am wearing are indeed very dirty. However, it is winter now. If I give you these rags to wash, the cold will trouble me. Therefore, I will not give them. If, however, you can wash and dry these rags for me by this evening, and give me that assurance, then take these rags, wash them, and return quickly." The Shiva devotee says this to Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. Why does he say it must be done by evening? Because at sunset, the cold intensifies. Therefore, he gives the clothes with the condition that they must be returned before the cold can cause distress. Furthermore, the Shiva devotee says, "Bring them quickly, wash them quickly and bring them." Then Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar says to the Shiva devotee, "Please give me these rags. I will wash and dry them without any delay for you to wear, and bring them before evening." Then the Shiva devotee says to Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, "If you do not quickly wash and dry these rags and return them today, you will have caused harm to this body," and he gives the rags. We must note the words spoken by the Shiva devotee here. We must note the place where Sekkizhar Peruman has placed the words spoken by the Shiva devotee. He says to Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, "You will have caused harm to this body." Suffering comes to the soul only through the body. Here, the Shiva devotee refers to "this body." It is the nature of great souls to not get entangled in karmic experiences but to limit those experiences to the body, to the extent of the body, and to exhaust all those sufferings and karmic experiences through the body. Therefore, it is customary for Shiva devotees and great souls to refer to "this body." Devotees separate their soul and body and attribute pleasures and pains to the body. It is the nature of great souls to stop all pleasures and pains at the level of the body and to think and speak without being affected by those pleasures and pains. Furthermore, the one who came as a Shiva devotee here is none other than God himself. Therefore, he says to Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, "You will have caused harm to this body that I have assumed." The inner meaning of this is that the trouble or suffering will affect "this body that I have assumed," and not me, the God within. Not only that, all the beings in this world are my body. Without being confined within them, I stand as the life within lives, taking beings as my body. The joys and sorrows that come to my body, which are beings, do not affect me – this is the inner meaning embedded in the philosophical truth. Having received the ragged cloth from the Shiva devotee, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar washes the cloth at one of the washing ghats in the water tank. In those days, there were separate areas in water tanks and rivers specifically for washermen to wash clothes. They would bring dirty clothes to those areas and wash them until all the dirt was gone. As a result of washing clothes, the dirty water would mix with the water in the tank and the surrounding areas. Therefore, the water in that area would not be suitable for drinking or bathing. Thus, they would allocate separate areas for washing clothes. Even today, in many places, one can find separate areas called 'Vannar Thurai' (washermen's ghat) in rivers and ponds, as written by Sivakavimani

Ayya. However, this good practice has been abandoned in the name of reform in these times, and people are polluting water bodies, rivers, and ponds in all possible ways, without any discipline or justice, causing distress to the world. This is regrettable, writes Sivakavimani Ayya. Sekkizhar Peruman describes Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar washing the Shiva devotee's rags with three phrases: 'maserindhu,' 'miga pulukki,' and 'piritholikka.' These three are important stages in removing dirt and whitening clothes. They are separate actions. First, the cloth must be rubbed to remove dirt. Then, soda ash must be applied to the cloth, and it must be steamed and whitened. After whitening with steam, the cloth must be thoroughly rinsed in water to remove both the dirt and the soda ash. These are the three stages of washing clothes. Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar performs these actions. As he performs the third action, which is steaming and whitening, and then rinsing the cloth to remove dirt and soda, noon passes, and evening arrives. Then, by the grace of Lord Shiva, heavy rain falls. This heavy rain is about to separate the Nayanar from this mortal world and unite him with Shiva's eternal world, the world of knowledge. The rain that came then was by the grace of Lord Shiva. Because, as we have already seen, the Nayanar's sacred star of liberation is Swathi in the month of Chithirai. At that time, in the month of Chithirai, rain is generally not expected. But if heavy rain falls during a time when it is not expected, we must understand that it happens by the grace of Lord Shiva. Furthermore, this falling rain appears to be an obstacle to the sacred service being performed by Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. Nevertheless, in reality, this rain that came was meant to unite the Nayanar with the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. Therefore, it becomes a rain of grace. How heavily and densely does the rain fall? The rain falls so densely that one's eyesight cannot penetrate the downpour. As this heavy rain falls, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's heart breaks as he remembers the promise he made to the devotee. He laments, "What shall I do now?" The devotee's sorrow and anxiety melt and grow even more. It causes a state that displaces the mental fortitude of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. As his mental fortitude is displaced, his intellect ceases to function. His mind is distressed by the situation where he cannot fulfill the promise he made. Therefore, he stands motionless, without mental or external action. Devotees are those whose state does not waver even if the five elements are disturbed. But now, the devotee is distressed, thinking, "Oh, the sacred service to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva's devotees has been troubled, an obstacle has arisen." He waits, thinking the continuous rain will stop. But the rain shows no signs of stopping. Then night also falls. As night falls, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, distressed that his sacred service to the ascetic whose body would suffer from the cold has failed, collapses to the ground. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 36 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 244. Mummaiyal Ulaganda Sarukkam. The Puranam of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. By the time Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar finished washing the Shiva devotee's ragged cloth, evening had arrived, heavy rain had fallen, and then night had also set in. He was distressed and saddened, thinking that the promise he had made to the Shiva devotee to wash and dry the cloth by that evening had been broken. He was disheartened and grieved that his sacred service to the devotee had been hindered, and that the devotee's body would suffer from the cold. His mind was so distraught that his strength to stand diminished, and he fell to the ground. The rain continued to fall without any sign of stopping. The time limit given by the true ascetic had passed. Because of this, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's heart was filled with anxiety. He lamented, "I failed to wash the cloth quickly beforehand and take it home to dry it indoors. I missed that opportunity." Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar was deeply pained. The rain was such that all the people of the world would pray for it, yet here, that very rain became an obstacle to the Nayanar's sacred service. He deeply regretted, "I could have washed the clothes before the rain, taken them to my house, and dried them in the air indoors without getting them wet. Why didn't that thought come to me?" Not only that, but he was now unable to return the cloth to the Shiva devotee. The ascetic already had an emaciated body. He lamented, "I have committed the evil of causing such an ascetic's body to suffer from the cold. What can I do now?" The Nayanar's lamentation here shows his deep involvement and melting devotion towards Shiva devotees, who embody the sacred form adorned with sacred ash. Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar intended only good; he never intended to harm the Shiva devotee. He did not harm the Shiva devotee, but he was distressed and blamed himself, thinking that harm had come to the ascetic because he had not returned the ragged cloth on time, and that he was responsible for it. As he grieved, Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar resolved to strike his head against the stone slab used for washing clothes, until his head was shattered. That stone slab, used for washing clothes, had previously been helpful to Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar in washing the clothes of Shiva devotees. Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's head and the body that bore his head had been instruments for washing the clothes of Shiva devotees. But now, his body had become the cause of harm to the devotee's sacred form. Therefore, the Nayanar resolved to strike his head against that stone slab and shatter it. Previously, that very slab had aided in the sacred service of devotees. Now, when harm came to the Shiva devotee's service, that same slab was also helping to provide a solution. Previously, this sturdy slab had helped in beating and washing the ragged clothes of Shiva devotees. Now, it also served as an aid when the Nayanar intended to strike his head against it. As he swiftly brought his head forward to strike it against the slab, the lotus-like red hand of Ekambaranathar, adorned with a beautiful bangle scar, emerged from the slab and caught the Nayanar's head. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this slab as a 'sezhumpaarai' (sturdy slab). Previously, this slab had helped in washing the ragged clothes of Shiva devotees. This same slab stood as an aid when the Nayanar intended to strike his head against it. And then, the sacred hand of Ekambaranathar emerged from that same slab to catch his head. Thus, that same

slab was the reason for the emergence of the Lord's sacred hand. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to it as a 'sezhumpaarai' (sturdy slab). He thinks in his mind of striking the slab. As soon as he thinks it, he performs the action. There is no delay whatsoever between his thought and the execution of the action. As he immediately resolved to strike his head, the sacred hand of Ekambaranathar caught his head. "If there is any desired gift, that too is your will," says the sixth verse of Kuzhaitta Pattu in Tiruvachagam. Accordingly, the action of the devotee and the action of the Lord occurred in unison. The sacred hand of God, as known from Tiruthandagam and other scriptures, and from the words of God in the Vedas, is omnipresent. Thus, the sacred hand of God is omnipresent. However, when it comes to love, its nature is to intensely manifest itself. The red hand emerged between the Nayanar's head and the slab before the Nayanar could strike his head against the slab. Not only did it emerge, but it caught the Nayanar's head. Just as the Lord's sacred hand caught the hand of Kannappa Nayanar in the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar, when he, seeing the wound, feared and tried to pluck out his second eye to apply it, saying, "The hand of the one who rides the bull caught the hand of the one who tried to pluck out his eye," similarly, here, the Lord's sacred hand caught the head of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. The Nayanar resolved to strike his head against the firm stone slab. Therefore, to support and hold his head, the Lord, with his lotus-like soft hand, caught and supported the Nayanar's head there. Just as he showed his firm sacred body to the Goddess by softening it, so too, with that softened body, with the bangle scar, he extends his lotus-like hand and supports the Nayanar's head. At that moment, the rain falling from the sky stops, and a shower of flowers falls. Lord Shiva, adorned with a garland of Konrai flowers on his sacred crown, appears in the sky on his Rishabha mount with Umaiyamman, giving darshan to Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. The rain that fell from the sky was a rain of grace that came to show compassion upon seeing the state of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. As soon as the Nayanar's sacred service was illuminated by grace, that rain also stopped. Then, whenever the divine grace of the Lord manifests, the Devas shower Kalpaka flowers. Lord Shiva, who a moment ago only extended his lotus-like hand to support the Nayanar's head, the omnipresent Lord Shiva, now appears in the sky on his Rishabha mount with Parvati, giving darshan to the Nayanar in the sky of knowledge. With abundant love overflowing in his heart, and all his senses melting, the Nayanar, who was previously in the rain of grace of the Tirodhana Shakti, now stands in the manifested rain of grace, the shower of flowers, and worships the Lord. Then, Lord Ekambaranathar says to Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, "We have made the three worlds know the state of your love. You shall henceforth remain forever in our eternal Shiva Loka, never to be separated." To bestow grace, the eye becomes the instrument. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the Lord here as the three-eyed one. Lord Shiva, with his three sacred eyes, bestowed full grace upon Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, the devotee who performed a great and difficult deed. The third eye is the eye of fire. It is the gaze that burned Manmatha. But here, it looked upon the Nayanar with direct grace, showering him with the rain of grace. Lord Ekambaranathar, who bestowed grace saying, "We have made your greatness known to the three worlds. Henceforth, you shall remain forever in Shiva Loka," then manifested from that state of appearance into his omnipresent state in Ekambaram, Kanchipuram. Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal, having sung the sacred history of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, praises the sacred service of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar and now begins to sing the sacred Puranam of Chandeswara Nayanar. Who can fully comprehend its greatness? No one can, saying this, Sekkizhar Swamigal concludes the Puranam of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar and now begins to sing the Puranam of Chandeswara Nayanar. We too shall continue to reflect on the sacred Puranam of Chandeswara Nayanar by the grace of God. May the grace of Devi Kamakshi, who performs Shiva Puja daily according to Shiva Agamas in the great city of Kanchi, flourish. Praise to the sacred feet of Tiruv Ekambaranathar. Praise to the sacred feet of Sivagnana Yogigal. Praise to the sacred feet of Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. Praise to the sacred feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Praise to the sacred feet of Nambiandar Nambigal. Praise to the sacred feet of Sekkizhar Peruman. Praise to the sacred feet of Sivakavimani Ayya, who graciously wrote the commentary on Periya Puranam. Thiruchitrambalam.
